\chapter{Regression Trees}\label{regression-trees}

In this section, we are going to discuss \textbf{tree-based} models for \textbf{regression}.

\textbf{Framework}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Response Variable: \(Y\in \mathbb{R}\) -- continuous
\item
  Features: \(X = (X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_p)\)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Regression trees} are a \emph{non-linear} and \emph{non-parametric} regression model. As we are going to discover, tree-based methods are non-parametric methods that \emph{recursively} partition the feature space into hyper-rectangular subsets, and make predictions on each subset. At the same time, they are non-parametric since they do not assume a fixed functional relationship between the response and the set of predictors. Instead the complexity of the model is controlled by hyper-parameters which make them quite flexible as models.

Below we can see a sketch of a regression tree for a \texttt{wines} data set in which we want to predict the \emph{quality} of the wine (a numerical outcome) versus \emph{wine characteristics} (a set of predictors):

We will restrict our focus to \emph{binary} partitions of the tree. In the example above, we first split the space in \textbf{two} regions, and model the response by the mean of \(Y\) in each region. \emph{At each step}, we choose the variable and split-point to achieve the best fit. Then, one or both regions are split into two more regions, and this process is continued, until \emph{some} stopping rule is applied. Specifically in our example, the first split rule is whether \texttt{alcohol} is less/greater than 10.85. Then, the region to the left for which \(\text{alcohol}<10.85\) is split based on \texttt{volatile.acidity} and value 0.2525, and the region to the right is split based on \texttt{alcohol} again and value 11.7417, and so on and so forth. When we stop, the predicted \texttt{quality} for the wine is the average of all the points in the respective region.

\textbf{Terminology}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Leaf Node}: Each leaf node corresponds to a rectangular-shaped region in the \emph{feature space}.
\item
  \textbf{Internal Node}: These nodes are parent nodes with two children -- left and right nodes.
\item
  A \textbf{split} involves a variable \(j\) and a particular split value \(s\).
\end{itemize}

Due to their flexibility tree-based methods are easy to interpret. They are also \emph{invariant} under any monotone transformation of predictors. The tree-based algorithms used to fit the trees also allow us to handle high-dimensional data without modifying the algorithm, and they are able to incorporate interactions between variables automatically and perform variable selection tasks.

Single-tree models do not have very high prediction accuracy which is why ensemble tree models have been introduced.

\section{Single-Tree Building}\label{single-tree-building}

As we discussed to build a single tree, one needs to recursively partition the predictor space. So, we start by \textbf{initializing the root} node and starting with all training data. Then, we choose a \textbf{splitting rule} consisting of a predictor \(X^{(j)}\) and a constant \(c\) that we use to split the data in the node:
\[\mathbf{1}_{\{X^{(j)} \leq c\}}\]

Then, we recursively apply the procedure on each child node:

We denote a node by \((t)\), the children nodes by \((t_L, t_R)\). The split will consist of the pair (var \(j\), value \(s\)), and in the end we have the leaves or terminal nodes.
When we stop, we \textbf{predict} each terminal node using within-node data. Every leaf node (i.e.~a rectangle region \(R_m \in \mathcal{X}\)) is assigned a constant for regression tree
\[\hat{f}(X) = \sum_m c_m \mathbf{1}_{\{X \in R_m\}}\]

So, the natural questions that arise are the following:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Where} to split the tree?
\item
  \textbf{When} to stop?
\item
  \textbf{How} to predict the response at each leaf node?
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Prediction at Leaf Nodes}\label{prediction-at-leaf-nodes}

As we saw before, each leaf node corresponds to region \(R_m\) that contains some samples. The \textbf{prediction} for a leaf node is \emph{defined} to be the \emph{average of the response variable \(Y\)} for the samples in \(R_m\), \(m=1, \ldots, M\):
\[\hat{f}(X) = \sum_{m=1}^{M} c_m \mathbf{1}_{\{ X \in R_m\}}\]
In the expression above, we need to determine the constants \(c_m\). So, we minimize the expression with respect to \(c_m\):
\[\min_{c_m} \sum_{i=1, x_i \in R_m}^{n} (y_i - c_m)^2\]
Thus, we obtain \textbf{\(c_m\) to be the average of \(y_i\)s that corresponds to \(x_i \in R_m\)}.

\subsection{Split Criterion}\label{split-criterion}

Finding the \textbf{best partition} in terms of minimum sum of squares is computationally infeasible which means that we need to use a greedy algorithm find the minimum. So, we start with all the data at the root, and the using a split variable \(j\) and a split point \(s\), we obtain the following two half planes:
\[R_1 (j,s) = \bigl\{X| X_j \leq s \bigr\} \quad \text{ and } \quad R_2 (j,s) = \bigl\{X| X_j > s \bigr\} \]
Then, we need to find the splitting variable \(j\) and split point \(s\) that solve
\[\min_{j,s} \Bigl[ \min_{c_1} \sum_{x_i \in R_1(j,s)} (y_i - c_1)^2 + \min_{c_2} \sum_{x_i \in R_2(j,s)} (y_i - c_2)^2 \Bigr]\]
For any choice of \(j\) and \(s\), the inner minimization is solved by:
\[ \hat{c}_1 = \text{ave} \Bigl(y_i | x_i \in R_1(j,s) \Bigr) \, \text{ and }  \hat{c}_2 = \text{ave} \Bigl(y_i | x_i \in R_2(j,s) \Bigr)\]

For each splitting variable, the determination of the split point \(s\) can be done quickly. So, the determination of the best pair is feasible.

The process we discuss above can be summarized in the following way: for each split, we define a \emph{split criterion} \(\Phi(j,s)\) e.g., we may consider the \textbf{deduction of RSS} for regression. By construction, regression trees are built in a \emph{top-down greedy fashion}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Start with the \emph{root} and consider

  \textbf{a.} all possible variables \(j=1, \ldots, p\) and

  \textbf{b.} all possible split values,

  \textbf{c.} and pick the best split, that is the one having the best \(\Phi\) value.
\item
  Now, the \emph{data are divided into the left node and right node}.
\item
  Repeat this procedure in each node.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Split Criterion \(\Phi(j, s)\)}

Deduction in RSS, if we split samples at node \(t\) into \(t_R\) and \(t_L\):
\[ \Phi(j, s) = RSS(t) - \Bigl[RSS(t_R) + RSS(t_L) \Bigr] \]
where
\[RSS(t) = \sum_{x_i \in t} (y_i - c_t)^2, \quad \text{ where } \quad c_t = \text{AVE}\{y_i : x_i \in t\}\]

Note that \(\Phi(j, s)\) is always positive if we split the data into two groups (even randomly), unless the mean of the left node and the one of right node are the same.

\subsubsection*{Categorical Predictors}\label{categorical-predictors}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Categorical Predictors}

If we have a categorical predictor with \(m\) levels, then this can be naturally handled by the tree model. Having \(m\) levels implies that there will be \(2^{m-1}-1\) possible partitions of the \(m\) labels into \emph{two} groups leading to a simplified computation for regression with a \emph{squared error loss}:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Order the \(m\) levels by their mean values of \(Y\),
\item
  Split the categorical variable as if it were an ordered predictor, so there will only be \((m-1)\) potential splits.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Missing Predictor Values}\label{missing-predictor-values}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Missing Predictor Values}

As we discussed in the beginning, tree-based model algorithms cal also handle missing values automatically. In general, if we discard observations with missing values, this will lead to a serious depletion of the training set. However, we can start by ignoring missing value observations, and determine the split criteria using only non-missing observations. Once a split \((j,s)\) is found, we can ``fill-in'' the missing values. This can be done, for example, using imputation techniques.

Alternatively, we can find \emph{surrogate} variables that can predict the binary outcome ``\(X_j < s\)'' and ``\(X_j \geq s\)'' using a \emph{one-split tree}. We rank those surrogate variables along with the blind rule ``go with majority''. Any observation that is missing \(X_j\) is then classified with the first surrogate variable, or if missing that, the second surrogate variable (or the blind rule) is used, and etc.

\subsection{Single-Tree Pruning}\label{single-tree-pruning}

So far we discussed how to predict the response in the leaves of the tree and we developed a split criterion. Thus, it remains to decide \emph{how and when we stop} which also determines the size of the tree.

To clarify, when we refer to the \emph{``size of a tree''} we refer to the \emph{number of splits} a tree has. A large tree (with many splits) can easily over-fit the data, but on the other hand it may capture important structures that a smaller tree might miss. So, intuitively balancing the tree size and accuracy resembles the ``loss + penalty'' framework.

One possible approach is to split tree nodes only if the decrease in the loss (e.g.~RSS) exceeds certain threshold, however this can be short-sighted, since what might seem as a useless split at this step might lead to a very good split below. So, we need to develop a \emph{different strategy}: We construct a large tree which we then \textbf{prune}.

We start by growing a very large tree \(T_{\max}\):

\begin{itemize}
\item
  until all terminal nodes are nearly pure, that is the variability of the points in that node is small.
\item
  or when the number of data in each terminal node is less than certain threshold;
\item
  or when the tree reaches a certain size.
\end{itemize}

The size of the initial tree is not critical as long as the tree is \emph{sufficiently large}.

\textbf{Notation}

Subtree \(T'\prec T\): any tree that can be obtained by pruning \(T\), i.e.~by collapsing all its internal nodes.
Branch at node \((t)\): \(T_t\)

For any subtree \(T \prec T_{\max}\) define the \textbf{complexity cost}
\[R_{\alpha}(T) = R(T) + \alpha|T|,\]
where \(R(T)\) is the RSS for the regression tree \(T\), \(|T|\) is the tree size, i.e.~the number of leaf nodes, and \(\alpha >0\) is the (\textbf{penalty}) cost of adding a split. Then, our goal is to find the value of \(\alpha\) that minimizes the complexity cost. Or, in other words, we want to pick the best subtree that minimizes the cost
\begin{align*}
T(\alpha) &= \arg\min_{T\preceq T_{\max}} R_{\alpha}(T) \\
&= \arg\min_{T\preceq T_{\max}} \Bigl[ R(T) + \alpha|T|\Bigr] 
\end{align*}

The \textbf{Optimal subtree \(T^{*}(\alpha)\)} is defined to be the smallest one among all \(T(\alpha)\)'s, that is
\[R_{\alpha}(T^{*}(\alpha)) = \min_{T\preceq T_{\max}} R_{\alpha}(T)\]
\[T^{*}(\alpha) \preceq \text{ any  } T(\alpha).\]
where \(T^{*}(\alpha)\) is unique.

Note that for a pair of leaf nodes \((t_L, t_R)\), there exists \(\alpha^{*}\), such that

\begin{itemize}
\item
  For any \(\alpha \geq \alpha^*\), we would like to collapse them to just node \(t\);
\item
  For any \(\alpha \leq \alpha^*\), we prefer to keep the two leaf nodes.
\end{itemize}

That is, \(\alpha^{*}\) is \emph{the maximal price we would like to pay to keep that split}. In a similar way, we can extend this calculation to compute \emph{the maximal price we would like to pay to keep a \textbf{branch}} \(T_t\). For any non-leaf node \(t\), we need to compute the maximal price we would like to pay for keeping the whole branch \(T_t\), focusing only on samples at node \(t\). So,

\begin{itemize}
\item
  The cost for keeping a branch \(T_t\):
  \[R_{\alpha}(T_t) = R(T_t) + \alpha |T_t|\]
\item
  The cost for cutting a branch \(T_t\):
  \[R_{\alpha}(\{t\}) = R(\{t\}) + \alpha \]
\end{itemize}

So, we calculate the difference between the two costs:
\[\alpha^{*} = \frac{R(\{t\})  - R(T_t)}{|T_t|-1}\]
This implies that if the given \(\alpha > \alpha^*\), then it is too expensive to keep this
branch and we would like to cut the whole branch and make \(t\) a leaf node.

The main disadvantage of this approach is that if we need to search all possible sub-trees of \(T_{\max}\), then this is computationally intense. However, this is not necessary. Below we introduce the \emph{Weakest Link Pruning} algorithm that finds and removes the least important branches of a large tree step-by-step to produce a sequence of smaller simpler trees. Specifically:

\textbf{Weakest Link Pruning Algorithm}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \emph{Start} with \(T_0=T_{\max}\) and \(\alpha_0=0\).
\item
  For any non-leaf node \(t\),
  \[\alpha(t) = \text{   the maximal price you would pay to keep } T_t \]
\item
  \(\alpha_1 = \min_t \alpha(t)\)
  The corresponding non-terminal node \(t_1\) is called the \textbf{weakest link}.
\item
  \emph{Cut} the branch at \(t_1\).
\item
  \emph{Update} the maximal price for each non-leaf node (we only need to recompute the maximal price for nodes that are the parents/grandparents of \(t_1\)).
\item
  \emph{Find} \(\alpha_2\) and cut the branch at the 2nd weakest link.\medskip
\item
  \emph{Keep} doing this until we get to the root.
\end{enumerate}

This algorithm generates a \textbf{Solution Path} in the following sense:

\[ T_{\max} = T_0 \succ T^{*}(\alpha_1) \succ T^{*}(\alpha_2) \succ \ldots \succ \{\text{root node}\}\]
\[0=\alpha_0 <\alpha_1 <\alpha_2 < \ldots\]
All possible values of \(\alpha\) are grouped into \((m+1)\) intervals
\begin{align*}
I_0 &= [0, \alpha_1)\\
I_1 &= [\alpha_1, \alpha_2)\\
\vdots &\\
I_m &= [\alpha_m, \infty)
\end{align*}
where all \(\alpha \in I_i\) share the same optimal subtree \(T^{*}(\alpha_i)\).

\subsubsection*{\texorpdfstring{Tuning of the hyper-parameter \(\alpha\)}{Tuning of the hyper-parameter \textbackslash alpha}}\label{tuning-of-the-hyper-parameter-alpha}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Tuning of the hyper-parameter \(\alpha\)}

We can use \(K\)-Fold Cross validation to tune \(\alpha\) -- the hyper-parameter in the pruning algorithm above. Therefore, we can start by fitting a very large tree \(T_{\max}\) and compute \(I_0, I_1, \ldots, I_m\) as above. Then, we can set
\begin{align*}
\beta_0 &= 0\\
\beta_1 &= \sqrt{\alpha_1 \alpha_2}\\
\vdots &\\
\beta_{m-1} &= \sqrt{\alpha_{m-1} \alpha_m}\\
\beta_m &= \infty
\end{align*}
where each \(\beta_j\) is a typical value for its interval \(I_j\).

To perform the \(K\) for cross-validation, we divide data into \(K\) groups and repeat \(k=1, \ldots, K\).
On each data set, except for the \(k\)-th group, we fit a full model and determine the optimal subtrees:
\[T_0 \succ T^{*}(\beta_1) \succ \ldots  \succ T^{*}(\beta_m) \succ \{\text{root node} \}\]
We then compute the prediction error on the \(k\)th group for each of the tree models. We finish by producing the CV plot over different \(\alpha\) values and pick the optimal \(\alpha\) via \(\alpha_{\min}\) or \(\alpha_{1se}\).

\section{Regression Forests}\label{regression-forests}

Regression forests naturalyl extend the idea of regression trees by combining many trees into an \textbf{ensemble}. Each tree is trained on a randomly samples subset of data features, which reduced correlation among trees and mitigates the overfitting of a single large tree. The final prediction is obtained by averaging out the outputs of the individual trees, leading to a higher accuracy, stability and generalization performance.

\subsection{Weak and Strong Learners}\label{weak-and-strong-learners}

In our discussion of single-tree models, we highlighted their main drawbacks: they are often unstable -- meaning that small small changes in the data can lead to very different trees -- and they tend to lack accuracy. For this reason they are considered \textbf{weak learners}.

A natural idea, widely explored in the literature, is to combine many weak learners to form a \textbf{strong learner}. Examples of such ensemble methods include bagging, boosting, and random forests.

Bagging and random forests construct multiple trees under different \emph{random perturbations}, and \emph{average} their predictions, while boosting takes a different approach; it \emph{progressively} trains simple models of small capacity, and then \emph{adds} them together.

Conceptually, an \textbf{ensemble} of trees is as follows:

\subsection{Bootstrap Aggregation (Bagging)}\label{bootstrap-aggregation-bagging}

Bagging (i.e.~for bootstrap aggregation) is an ensemble method designed to reduce the variance of a predictive model---particularly unstable ones. It works by repeatedly \emph{resampling} the training data \emph{with replacement}, fitting a model on each bootstrap sample, and then averaging their predictions (\emph{for regression}) or taking a majority vote (\emph{for classification}).

Consider having Training Data denoted by \(\mathbf{Z} = \Bigl\{(x_i, y_i)_{i=1}^{n} \Bigr\}\). Then, we can obtain \textbf{bootstrap samples with replacement} \(\mathbf{Z}^{*b} = \Bigl \{(x_i^{*b}, y_i^{*b})_{i=1}^{n} \Bigr \}\), where \(b=1, \ldots, B\), that is
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{Z}^{*1} &: (x_1^{*1}, y_1^{*1}), (x_2^{*1}, y_2^{*1}), \ldots, (x_n^{*1}, y_n^{*1})\\
\mathbf{Z}^{*2} &: (x_1^{*2}, y_1^{*2}), (x_2^{*2}, y_2^{*2}), \ldots, (x_n^{*2}, y_n^{*2})\\
\vdots &\\
\mathbf{Z}^{*B}   &: (x_1^{*B}, y_1^{*B}), (x_2^{*B}, y_2^{*B}), \ldots, (x_n^{*B}, y_n^{*B})
\end{align*}

Then, the regression (or classification) function, \(\hat{f}^{*b}\), is trained on each sample \(\mathbf{Z}^{*b}\) and the \textbf{Bagging Estimate} is defined to be
\[\hat{f}_{bag}(x) = \frac{1}{B} \sum_{b=1}^{B} \hat{f}^{*b}(x)\]

Because bagging relies on averaging the predictions of many individual trees, it can substantially reduce the variance of otherwise unstable learners like trees, thus improving predictive performance. However, the simple and interpretable structure of a single tree is lost once it is embedded in an ensemble. Moreover, if the individual trees are highly correlated, the variance reduction achieved through bagging may be limited.

\subsection{Random Forests}\label{random-forests}

The development of random forests can be seen as the convergence of several ideas that emerged in the 1990s. One major breakthrough was Breiman's introduction of bagging (bootstrap aggregation) that we discussed above, which demonstrated that averaging unstable predictors like trees could dramatically improve their performance by reducing variance. Around the same time, Ho proposed the random subspace method (1998), showing that further \emph{diversity} among trees could be achieved by training each one on a randomly selected subset of features. This insight highlighted that injecting randomness into feature selection, not just the data, could strengthen ensembles of trees. Breiman built on both of these advances when he introduced \textbf{random forests}: a method that combines \emph{bagging} with \emph{random feature selection} at every split. By doing so, random forests create a large collection of de-correlated trees whose averaged predictions are \emph{both accurate and stable}. This synthesis of bagging and random subspaces represents a turning point in ensemble learning, establishing random forests as one of the most powerful and widely used machine learning methods.

The random forest algorithm can be described in a few key steps:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Bootstrap Sampling (Bagging): For each tree in the forest, a bootstrap sample of the training data is drawn -- this introduces variability among the trees.
\item
  Tree Construction with Random Feature Selection: Each tree is grown on its bootstrap sample, but with an important twist: at each split in the tree, only a randomly chosen subset of features is considered as candidates for the split -- his further decorrelates the trees and enhances the variance reduction achieved by averaging.
\item
  Tree Growth: Typically, the trees in a random forest are grown deep (without pruning), allowing them to capture complex patterns in the data. Overfitting of individual trees is not a concern, since averaging across many trees stabilizes the final model.
\item
  Prediction: For regression, the forest's prediction is the average of the predictions from all trees.
\item
  Out-of-Bag (OOB) Error Estimation:
  Since each bootstrap sample leaves out about one-third of the training data, these ``out-of-bag'' observations can be used to estimate prediction error, providing a built-in form of cross-validation.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Sketch of the Algorithm}

For \(b=1, \ldots, B\)

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Draw a bootstrap sample of size \(n\) \(\mathbf{Z}^{*b}\) from the training data.
\item
  Grow a BIG tree \(T_b\) by recursively repeating the following steps for each terminal node of the tree, until the minimum node size is reached:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{verbatim}
1. Select m variables at random from the p variables.

2. Pick the best variable/split-point among the m.

3. Split the node into two children nodes.
\end{verbatim}

Output the forest, i.e.~\emph{ensemble} of trees: \(\{T_b\}_{b=1}^{B}\)

Predict at a new point \(x\) (Regression case)
\[ \hat{f}^B (x) = \frac{1}{B} \sum_{b=1}^{B} T_b(x)\]

\subsubsection*{Restrictions}\label{restrictions}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Restrictions}

When constructing each tree in the forest, at every split, we consider a subset of \(m\) features is chosen at random from the total of \(p\) available features. The algorithm then determines the best split using only this subset of \(m\) features. A common rule of thumb is to set \(m=\sqrt{p}\) for classification and \(m=p/3\) for regression.

By restricting each split to a random subset of features, the trees are less likely to all rely on the same strong predictors, thereby reducing correlation among trees and increasing the overall effectiveness of the ensemble.

\subsection{Out-of-Bag (OOB) Samples}\label{out-of-bag-oob-samples}

Out-of-bag (OOB) samples are the observations that do not appear in the bootstrap dataset \(Z^{*b}\) and are therefore not used to build the corresponding tree \(T_b\). These left-out samples provide a natural test set for evaluating \(T_b\). In practice, the OOB error is computed by aggregating predictions for each observation using only the trees where that observation was excluded from training. Implementations such as \texttt{randomForest} report predictions and error rates based on this OOB evaluation. The resulting error estimate is typically very close to what would be obtained through cross-validation.

\subsubsection{Variable Importance}\label{variable-importance}

Variable Importance in Random Forests can be assessed in two main ways. Based on:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Reduction in RSS (or Impurity):
  At each split, the algorithm records the improvement in the residual sum of squares (RSS) that results from splitting on a given variable. This improvement is attributed to the splitting variable. For each variable, these improvements are accumulated over all splits in a tree, and then averaged across all trees in the forest. The result reflects how much each variable contributes to reducing prediction error through the splitting process.
\item
  Permutation of Predictors:
  For each tree \(T_b\), compute its prediction error on the out-of-bag (OOB) samples (mean squared error for regression, classification error for classification). Next, randomly permute the values of the \(j\)th predictor within the OOB data and recalculate the prediction error. The increase in error caused by this permutation indicates the importance of that variable. This difference is averaged across all trees and, to account for variability, further normalized by the corresponding standard deviation.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Boosting Trees}\label{boosting-trees}

Boosting is an ensemble learning technique that combines multiple weak learners, such as trees, into a single, strong predictive model. Unlike bagging or random forests, which grow trees independently, boosting builds trees \textbf{sequentially}, with each new tree focused on correcting the errors of the previous ensemble.

For example, if we consider an additive model,
\[F(x) = f_1(x) + f_2(x) + \ldots + f_{T-1}(x) + f_T(x)\]
it is difficult to solve for all \(f_t\)s. Instead we solve it using a forward stagewise greedy algorithm.

Boosting leads to very high predictive accuracy, often outperforming bagging methods, and it is flexible and capable of capturing complex nonlinear relationships. However, it is more sensitive to noise and outliers compared to bagging methods, and it requires careful tuning of hyperparameters to avoid overfitting.

\section{Tree-Based Regression Models in R}\label{tree-based-regression-models-in-r}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(rpart)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Attaching package: 'rpart'
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## The following object is masked from 'package:faraway':
## 
##     solder
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(rpart.plot)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(tree)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{whitewines.csv} data set contains information related to white variants of the Portuguese `\texttt{Vinho\ Verde"\ wine.\ \ \textless{}br\textgreater{}\textless{}br\textgreater{}\ Specifically,\ we\ have\ recorded\ the\ following\ information:\ \ (a)}fixed acidity\texttt{,\ \ (b)}volatile acidity\texttt{,\ \ (c)}citric acid\texttt{,\ \ (d)}residual sugar\texttt{,\ \ (e)}chlorides\texttt{,\ \ (f)}free sulfur dioxide\texttt{,\ \ (g)}total sulfur dioxide\texttt{,\ \ (h)}density\texttt{,\ \ (i)}pH\texttt{,\ \ (j)}sulphates\texttt{,\ \ (k)}alcohol\texttt{,\ \ (l)}quality` (score between 0 and 10)
The data set can be downloaded here: whitewines.csv

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{whitewines }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{read.table}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/week6/whitewines.csv"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sep=}\StringTok{";"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{header=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(whitewines)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##   fixed.acidity volatile.acidity citric.acid residual.sugar chlorides
## 1           7.0             0.27        0.36           20.7     0.045
## 2           6.3             0.30        0.34            1.6     0.049
## 3           8.1             0.28        0.40            6.9     0.050
## 4           7.2             0.23        0.32            8.5     0.058
## 5           7.2             0.23        0.32            8.5     0.058
## 6           8.1             0.28        0.40            6.9     0.050
##   free.sulfur.dioxide total.sulfur.dioxide density   pH sulphates alcohol
## 1                  45                  170  1.0010 3.00      0.45     8.8
## 2                  14                  132  0.9940 3.30      0.49     9.5
## 3                  30                   97  0.9951 3.26      0.44    10.1
## 4                  47                  186  0.9956 3.19      0.40     9.9
## 5                  47                  186  0.9956 3.19      0.40     9.9
## 6                  30                   97  0.9951 3.26      0.44    10.1
##   quality
## 1       6
## 2       6
## 3       6
## 4       6
## 5       6
## 6       6
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{dim}\NormalTok{(whitewines)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 4898   12
\end{verbatim}

In \texttt{R} there are two packages that fit \textbf{tree} models: \texttt{tree} and \texttt{rpart}. The first package we only used it to generate the first figures in the slides via its function \texttt{partition.tree}. Below is the code that generates the plots in the lectures.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{The \texttt{tree} Package}{The tree Package}}\label{the-tree-package}

The syntax for fitting a regression tree with tree is similar to that used for linear regression models. For illustration purposes, let's focus on just two predictors: \texttt{alcohol} and \texttt{volatile.acidity} to predict \texttt{quality}.

We start by fitting the tree:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{quality.tree }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{tree}\NormalTok{(quality }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ alcohol }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ volatile.acidity, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{whitewines)}
\NormalTok{quality.tree}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## node), split, n, deviance, yval
##       * denotes terminal node
## 
##  1) root 4898 3841.00 5.878  
##    2) alcohol < 10.85 3085 1845.00 5.606  
##      4) volatile.acidity < 0.2525 1475  868.00 5.873 *
##      5) volatile.acidity > 0.2525 1610  775.30 5.361 *
##    3) alcohol > 10.85 1813 1378.00 6.341  
##      6) alcohol < 11.7417 881  670.00 6.121  
##       12) volatile.acidity < 0.465 865  616.50 6.150 *
##       13) volatile.acidity > 0.465 16   13.94 4.562 *
##      7) alcohol > 11.7417 932  624.70 6.549 *
\end{verbatim}

To illustrate the tree structure, we plot the fitted tree model:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(quality.tree)}
\FunctionTok{text}\NormalTok{(quality.tree, }\AttributeTok{cex =}\NormalTok{ .}\DecValTok{75}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-165-1.pdf}}

and create the rectangular regions (as in the lecture), we have:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{quality.quantiles }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{cut}\NormalTok{(whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality, }\FunctionTok{unique}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality, }\DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:} \DecValTok{5} \SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)),}
  \AttributeTok{include.lowest =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{alcohol, whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{volatile.acidity, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{grey}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5}\SpecialCharTok{:} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{)[quality.quantiles],}
  \AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"volatile.acidity"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"alcohol"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{partition.tree}\NormalTok{(quality.tree, }\AttributeTok{ordvars =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"alcohol"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"volatile.acidity"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{add =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-166-1.pdf}}

If we want, we can also prune the tree using the \texttt{prune.tree} function:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{smaller.tree }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{prune.tree}\NormalTok{(quality.tree, }\AttributeTok{best=}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{smaller.tree}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## node), split, n, deviance, yval
##       * denotes terminal node
## 
## 1) root 4898 3841.0 5.878  
##   2) alcohol < 10.85 3085 1845.0 5.606  
##     4) volatile.acidity < 0.2525 1475  868.0 5.873 *
##     5) volatile.acidity > 0.2525 1610  775.3 5.361 *
##   3) alcohol > 10.85 1813 1378.0 6.341  
##     6) alcohol < 11.7417 881  670.0 6.121 *
##     7) alcohol > 11.7417 932  624.7 6.549 *
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(smaller.tree)}
\FunctionTok{text}\NormalTok{(smaller.tree, }\AttributeTok{cex =}\NormalTok{ .}\DecValTok{75}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-167-1.pdf}}

We now detach the \texttt{tree} package and switch to the \texttt{rpart} package.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{detach}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"package:tree"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{The \texttt{rpart} Package}{The rpart Package}}\label{the-rpart-package}

\subsubsection*{Fitting a Regression Tree}\label{fitting-a-regression-tree}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Fitting a Regression Tree}

The syntax for fitting a regression tree with \texttt{rpart} to the one we use for linear regression models. The default tree plot may not be visually appealing, so for better visualization, we can use the \texttt{rpart.plot} package.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{598}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{wine.tree1 }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{rpart}\NormalTok{(quality}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{., }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ whitewines)}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1)}
\FunctionTok{rpart.plot}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-169-1.pdf}}
\emph{Note} that in this example (unlike the previous one), we use all predictors not just two.

\subsubsection*{Understanding the Fitted Tree}\label{understanding-the-fitted-tree}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Understanding the Fitted Tree}

Starting at the root node, the average value for all observations is \textbf{5.9}, representing 100\% of the data points. The \emph{first split} occurs based on the variable \texttt{alcohol}. The left node contains roughly 63\% of the samples with an average of \textbf{5.6}, while the right node contains the remaining 37\% with an average of \textbf{6.3}. Leaf nodes appear at the bottom, and their color shading indicates the magnitude of their predictions.

\textbf{Cross-Validation}

\textbf{Cross-validation} is automatically performed when using the \texttt{rpart} command. To inspect the cross-validation for pruning, use the \texttt{printcp} command, which provides a table.

\emph{CP} stands for \textbf{Complexity Parameter} which is a scaled version of \(\alpha\) -- the \emph{price} or \emph{penalty} we pay for keeping a split.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{printcp}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Regression tree:
## rpart(formula = quality ~ ., data = whitewines)
## 
## Variables actually used in tree construction:
## [1] alcohol             density             free.sulfur.dioxide
## [4] volatile.acidity   
## 
## Root node error: 3841/4898 = 0.7842
## 
## n= 4898 
## 
##         CP nsplit rel error  xerror     xstd
## 1 0.161007      0   1.00000 1.00030 0.021275
## 2 0.052469      1   0.83899 0.84260 0.020094
## 3 0.027342      2   0.78652 0.79453 0.019601
## 4 0.017711      3   0.75918 0.76429 0.018478
## 5 0.010355      4   0.74147 0.75134 0.018065
## 6 0.010000      6   0.72076 0.74656 0.017995
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{In the output above:}
\texttt{nsplit} means the number of splits, which implies that the number of leaf nodes (or size of a tree) is equal to \(nsplit + 1\).
\texttt{rel\ error} and \texttt{xerror} are the training error (RSS) and CV error, but these values are relative to a reference training error at the root node. Recall that the connection between \(\alpha\) and CP is

\[RSS(T) = \alpha |T|,  \frac{RSS(T)}{RSS(root)} + CP \cdot |T|\]

\textbf{Pruning}

We can employ the \texttt{prune} command to obtain the optimal subtree by providing specific Complexity Parameter (CP) values. As an example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{prune}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1, }\AttributeTok{cp =} \FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## n= 4898 
## 
## node), split, n, deviance, yval
##       * denotes terminal node
## 
## 1) root 4898 3840.99 5.877909 *
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{prune}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1, }\AttributeTok{cp =} \FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## n= 4898 
## 
## node), split, n, deviance, yval
##       * denotes terminal node
## 
## 1) root 4898 3840.99 5.877909 *
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{prune}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1, }\AttributeTok{cp =} \FloatTok{0.156}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## n= 4898 
## 
## node), split, n, deviance, yval
##       * denotes terminal node
## 
## 1) root 4898 3840.990 5.877909  
##   2) alcohol< 10.85 3085 1844.906 5.605511 *
##   3) alcohol>=10.85 1813 1377.659 6.341423 *
\end{verbatim}

A CV path plot

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plotcp}\NormalTok{(wine.tree1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-172-1.pdf}}

If you are uncertain whether the cross-validation error has reached its minimum, you can start with a larger tree:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{wine.tree2 }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{rpart}\NormalTok{(quality}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{., }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ whitewines, }\AttributeTok{control =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{cp =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xval =} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{))}
\CommentTok{\#printcp(wine.tree2)}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-173-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{plotcp}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-174-1.pdf}}

\textbf{Optimal CP Value}

We can use either the \emph{Minimum Cross-Validation error} or the \emph{One Standard Error (1-SE)} rule to choose the \textbf{optimal CP} value for pruning.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Index of CP with lowest xerror}
\NormalTok{optimal.cp }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{which.min}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[, }\StringTok{"xerror"}\NormalTok{]) }

\CommentTok{\# The optimal CP value}
\NormalTok{wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.6961474
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# upper bound for equivalent optimal xerror}
\NormalTok{wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.713746
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Row IDs for CPs whose xerror are equivalent to min(xerror)}
\NormalTok{tmp.id }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{which}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=}\NormalTok{ wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[optimal.cp, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{tmp.id }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{min}\NormalTok{(tmp.id)}

\CommentTok{\# CP.1se = any value between row(tmp.id) and(tmp.id {-} 1)}
\NormalTok{CP}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{se }\OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ (wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[tmp.id }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ wine.tree2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cptable[tmp.id, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}

\CommentTok{\# Prune tree with CP.1se}
\NormalTok{wine.tree3 }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{prune}\NormalTok{(wine.tree2, }\AttributeTok{cp =}\NormalTok{ CP}\FloatTok{.1}\NormalTok{se)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let us now try to understand the relationship between the \emph{1st column} and the \emph{3rd column} of the CP table. The difference between adjacent rel error in the table is equivalent to the corresponding CP values. This difference signifies the \textbf{improvement gained in RSS by adding an additional split}, effectively setting the \emph{cost} of that split.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\#cbind(wine.tree2$cptable[, 1], c({-}diff(wine.tree2$cptable[, 3]), 0))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

After the tree is fit, the predict function \texttt{predict.rpart} from the \texttt{rpart} package can be used for making predictions on new or existing data.

\section*{Random Forests in R}\label{random-forests-in-r}

We start by loading the random forest package in \texttt{R}. We also split the data in training and testing data sets.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(randomForest)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## randomForest 4.7-1.2
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## Type rfNews() to see new features/changes/bug fixes.
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## 
## Attaching package: 'randomForest'
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## The following object is masked from 'package:ggplot2':
## 
##     margin
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{n }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(whitewines)}
\NormalTok{ntest }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(n}\SpecialCharTok{*}\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{598}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{test.id }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{sample}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n, ntest)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection*{Model Fitting}\label{model-fitting}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Model Fitting}

To fit the model, we use the \texttt{randomForest} function. Our goal is to predict the \texttt{quality} using all the other columns in the data matrix as potential predictors.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rf.wines }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{randomForest}\NormalTok{(quality }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ ., }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ whitewines[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id, ], }\AttributeTok{importance =}\NormalTok{ T, }\AttributeTok{ntree=}\DecValTok{400}\NormalTok{); }
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{randomForest} function outputs several quantities. Let us focus on \texttt{mtry} which is the number of variables selected for each split.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rf.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{mtry}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 3
\end{verbatim}

In our example, \texttt{mtry} is set 3, aligning with the recommended value for regression-based Random Forest models: \(p/3\), where \(p\) is the number of predictors.

We can use the fitted model to make predictions, and we can calculate the average test error:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{yhat.test }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{predict}\NormalTok{(rf.wines, whitewines[test.id, ]) }

\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ yhat.test)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(test.id)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.411837
\end{verbatim}

If we think about the \emph{training} error, then the random forest approach leads us to two types of training errors: one based on the prediction function (using the training data) and the other one based on the out-of-bag samples.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{yhat.train }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{predict}\NormalTok{(rf.wines, whitewines[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id, ])}
\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ yhat.train) }\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ ntest)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.07441902
\end{verbatim}

We can see that the training error is significantly smaller compared to the prediction error.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ rf.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{predicted) }\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ ntest)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## [1] 0.3726565
\end{verbatim}

The error based on the out-of-bag sample is closer to the testing error and a more reliable estimator to the model's generalization performance.
We can also see how many times an observation was in an out-of-bag sample (below we only output the first 10 observatios for illustration purposes):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rf.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{oob.times[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##  [1] 132 141 166 131 151 150 142 157 143 137
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection*{Performance Plots}\label{performance-plots}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Performance Plots}

We can visualize the performance of the random forest fitted model

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(rf.wines)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-184-1.pdf}}

The function is plotting the MSE values derived from out-of-bag predictions. The plot shows that the model's performance improves rapidly with the initial addition of trees but starts to plateau beyond a certain point. This suggests that adding more trees does not lead to overfitting, although one could potentially achieve similar performance with fewer trees.

\subsubsection*{Variable Importance}\label{variable-importance-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Variable Importance}

In the \texttt{randomForest} function we specified \texttt{importance} as `TRUE\textasciitilde{} which means that we can extract an importance table for the variables.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rf.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{importance}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
##                         %IncMSE IncNodePurity
## fixed.acidity        0.06555382      160.5398
## volatile.acidity     0.16982629      269.3357
## citric.acid          0.08526387      181.1612
## residual.sugar       0.14416479      193.1863
## chlorides            0.11123063      209.8724
## free.sulfur.dioxide  0.13832758      267.2291
## total.sulfur.dioxide 0.09535885      192.1764
## density              0.24062906      295.2742
## pH                   0.08382337      174.5245
## sulphates            0.04954697      155.4755
## alcohol              0.36331159      413.3186
\end{verbatim}

The first column \texttt{\%IncMSE} shows importance based on accuracy gain before/after permutation. The second column \texttt{IncNodePurity} shows importance based on RSS gain.
Both scaled and unscaled versions of these measures are available, along with their standard errors.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{importance}\NormalTok{(rf.wines, }\AttributeTok{scale =}\NormalTok{ F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

\section*{Gradient Boosting Machines in R}\label{gradient-boosting-machines-in-r}

Before running a GBM model, we need to tune the following hyperparameters:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Loss Function: For regression tasks (like here) we use the squared error

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \texttt{n.trees}: number of trees (default = 100)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \texttt{shrinkage}: shrinkage factor or learning rate (default = 0.1)
\item
  \texttt{bag.fraction}: fraction of the training data used for learning (default = 0.5)
\item
  \texttt{cv.folds}: number of folds for cross-validation (default = 0 - i.e.~no cv error returned)
\item
  \texttt{interaction.depth}: depth of individual trees (default = 1)
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(gbm)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## Loaded gbm 2.2.2
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
## This version of gbm is no longer under development. Consider transitioning to gbm3, https://github.com/gbm-developers/gbm3
\end{verbatim}

Fitting a GBM:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gbm.wines }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{gbm}\NormalTok{(quality }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ ., }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{whitewines[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{test.id, ], }\AttributeTok{distribution =} \StringTok{"gaussian"}\NormalTok{,}
                \AttributeTok{n.trees =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{shrinkage =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{interaction.depth =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bag.fraction =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}
                \AttributeTok{cv.folds =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{gbm.wines}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
## gbm(formula = quality ~ ., distribution = "gaussian", data = whitewines[-test.id, 
##     ], n.trees = 100, interaction.depth = 3, shrinkage = 1, bag.fraction = 1, 
##     cv.folds = 5)
## A gradient boosted model with gaussian loss function.
## 100 iterations were performed.
## The best cross-validation iteration was 17.
## There were 11 predictors of which 11 had non-zero influence.
\end{verbatim}

We plot the CV error to find the optimal number of trees to prevent overfitting:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{opt.size }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{gbm.perf}\NormalTok{(gbm.wines)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-190-1.pdf}}

We can also evaluate the model's performance on the test data against the number of trees.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{size }\OtherTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{gbm.wines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{n.trees}
\NormalTok{test.err }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(size))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{(i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(size))\{}
\NormalTok{    y.pred }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{predict}\NormalTok{(gbm.wines, whitewines[test.id, ], }\AttributeTok{n.trees =}\NormalTok{ size[i])}
\NormalTok{    test.err[i] }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((whitewines}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{quality[test.id] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ y.pred)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}    }
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(test.err, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{lines}\NormalTok{(size, test.err, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =}\NormalTok{ opt.size, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"blue"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{my-598PSL_files/figure-latex/unnamed-chunk-191-1.pdf}}

We can change the tuning parameters and fit a new GBM:

                \AttributeTok{n.trees =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{shrinkage =} \FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{ , }\AttributeTok{interaction.depth =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bag.fraction =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cv.folds =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
